# Regional Anesthesia in Children

<!-- PDF page 1 -->


BAN TSUI and SANTHANAM SURESH

### KEY POINTS

- Regional anesthesia has become routine and an integral part of everyday pediatric anesthesia practice as a viable option for treating children’s intraoperative and postoperative pain control.

- The blocks can be performed with greater certainty and ease if the clinician is familiar with the anatomic relationship between the nerves, surrounding bone, and blood vessels.

- Growing evidence supports the routine use of peripheral catheter techniques in pediatric patients. Data from large pediatric databases have bolstered the safety of pediatric regional anesthesia. Improved pain management and facilitated home care are just some of the benefits of these regional anesthesia techniques, which also have the potential to allow for earlier ambulation and enhanced recovery in children.

- Ultrasound guidance gradually replaces landmark techniques and nerve stimulation localization approaches in pediatric regional anesthesia. The advantages of the ultrasoundguided regional technique include visualization of targeted nerves and spaces, the spread of injected local anesthetic, and improved block safety.

- With the advent of ultrasound technology, interfascial plane blocks have entered a new era that was previously impossible.

- Using ultrasound guidance for peripheral nerve blocks allows for a reduction in local anesthetic doses, a reduction in the number of punctures, and a shortened onset time and prolonged duration of the sensitive block.

- To avoid local or systemic toxicity, the dose of local anesthetic should be calculated in volume and concentration. The use of lipid rescue (i.e., intralipid) has reduced the number of serious complications associated with regional anesthesia.

- Throughout this chapter, we have employed the most up- to- date, standardized nomenclature of regional blocks as advocated by the American Society of Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine (ASRA) and the European Society of Regional Anesthesia and Pain Therapy (ESRA).

## Introduction

Regional anesthesia for children and the study of its techniques and applications have followed its use in adults. Pediatric anesthesiologists have adopted numerous welldescribed blocks for use in children, giving clinicians confidence to explore the possibility of using them with even very low- birth- weight neonates. Large datasets show a high safety rate and a low risk of neurologic complications for pediatric patients receiving regional anesthesia.<sup>1</sup> Central and peripheral blocks performed on pediatric patients undergoing general anesthesia do not pose a heightened risk.<sup>2</sup> Additionally, Enhanced Recovery After Surgery (ERAS) protocols are becoming widely accepted as a standardized method for administering postoperative analgesia in children.

With the introduction of ultrasound guidance, numerous novel regional anesthetic techniques, including interfascial plane blocks, have been made possible. In light of the exponential increase in the number of different types of blocks, the American Society of Regional Anesthesia and Pain Medicine (ASRA) and the European Society of Regional Anaesthesia and Pain Therapy (ESRA) have both called for a new, standardized nomenclature of regional

anesthetic procedures.<sup>3</sup> Furthermore, a recent Cochrane review showed that ultrasound guidance is effective and can decrease the local anesthetic used for regional techniques.<sup>4</sup>

## Indications, Contraindications, and Complications

### INDICATIONS

Indications for regional anesthesia in children differ from those in adults, not only because surgical pathologic processes in children and adults are vastly different but also because regional block procedures are used as analgesia techniques in anesthetized children instead of conscious or lightly sedated adult patients. There are sufficient current data to indicate that regional anesthetic can be safely administered to children while they are asleep.<sup>5,6</sup>

### Anesthetic Indications

Interest in developing regional techniques for surgical anesthesia as an alternative to general anesthesia in children has increased in recent years.<sup>7</sup> This is because experimental

<!-- PDF page 2 -->

animals explored in general anesthetics have been shown to exhibit neurotoxicity, neuronal cell death, and behavioral and cognitive abnormalities. Extensive human cohort studies with bias have discovered a link between early general anesthesia and neurodevelopmental deficits. Some researchers, on the other hand, found no association. Despite the widespread hope that using awake regional anesthesia instead of early infancy general anesthesia could improve neurodevelopmental outcomes, a study with a predominantly male study population found that less than 1 hour of general anesthesia in early infancy did not affect the neurodevelopmental outcome at five years compared to awake- regional anesthesia.<sup>7</sup>

Regional anesthesia as the sole anesthesia in children and adolescents who are fully conscious is a possibility for other causes. Certain regional blocks can offer adequate surgical anesthesia and analgesia for short- duration surgery. Such a management approach may be explored for children at risk of severe complications under general anesthesia due to particular conditions, such as the following<sup>8</sup> :

- NPO ( _nil per os_ : nothing by mouth) violation with emergent surgery (e.g., testicular torsion or incarcerated hernia at immediate risk for rupture).

- Former preterm infants younger than 60 weeks’ postconceptional age who are at risk for severe postoperative apnea with general anesthesia (e.g., inguinal hernia repair).

- Acute or chronic respiratory failure.

- Emergencies in children with severe metabolic or endocrine issues.

- Neuromuscular diseases, myasthenia gravis, or some porphyrias.

- Skeletal malformations and congenital syndromes.

- Cervical instability (e.g., children with Chiari malformation, achondroplasia, and Down syndrome) makes tracheal intubation risky for tetraplegia.

- Difficult airway (e.g., children with facial abnormalities, microstomia, metabolic illnesses including Hurler and Hunter syndromes, and mandibular hypoplasia) makes general anesthesia less safe.

- Epidermolysis bullosa infants are difficult to manage under general anesthesia. Thus, regional block may be a safer alternative.<sup>9–11</sup>

- Trauma patients with extremity lesions may benefit from peripheral nerve block to relieve pain, allow wound dressing and temporary fracture stabilization, and monitor and evaluate head trauma and hemodynamic abnormalities, if care is taken to monitor compartment syndromes (see later).

### Management of Perioperative Pain

Nevertheless, analgesia remains the principal rationale for regional blocks in children because they give the highest risk–benefit ratio for numerous outpatient and inpatient surgeries, including orthopedic (such as scoliosis surgery), thoracic, urologic, and upper and lower abdominal surgeries.<sup>12–14</sup>

Cardiac surgery is a more controversial indication,<sup>15,16</sup> and many anesthesiologists are reluctant to perform neuraxial blocks in children scheduled to receive anticoagulants. However, this is changing with more recent guidelines

from the ASRA regarding heparinization after 60 minutes of placing the block. Regional anesthesia has been increasingly used in heart surgery, notably interfascial plane blocks. Interfascial cardiac regional anesthetic reduces opioid use intraoperatively and postoperatively.<sup>17</sup> Regional anesthesia utilization depends on anesthesiologists’ experience, consultative pain care, and other providers. Multiple studies show that pediatric congenital heart surgery under regional anesthetic is safe.

Commonly used erector spinae plane blocks and parasternal intercostal blocks with continuous infusions are safe, although their efficacy in enhancing outcomes has yet to be clearly demonstrated. Spinal anesthesia reduces postoperative opioid use and discomfort. Caudal analgesia for postoperative pain in children with normal coagulation profiles is reasonable, although trials comparing it to children without regional anesthesia had conflicting outcome benefits. Regional anesthesia may benefit certain patients, but further research is needed.<sup>17</sup> In a systematic review and meta- analysis of randomized clinical trials,<sup>18</sup> the effects of regional analgesia approaches and systemic analgesia on postoperative pain, nausea and vomiting, resource use, reoperation, mortality, and complications were compared in children undergoing heart surgery. Regional anesthesia reduces postoperative discomfort in children undergoing cardiac surgery for up to 24 hours. Regional analgesia during congenital heart surgery has not reduced severe morbidity or mortality. The findings should be interpreted cautiously since this is a meta- analysis of multiple small studies.

Minor procedural pain can easily be anticipated and thus prevented mostly by topical anesthesia or infiltration techniques.<sup>19–21</sup> The indications for perineural catheters depend on the expected duration of postoperative pain.<sup>22</sup> Also, surgery associated with intense postoperative pain (major orthopedic surgery, hand or foot amputation) and postoperative pain management or painful physical therapy necessary for several days (knee arthroscopy and repair) are excellent indications for catheters.<sup>23</sup>

A comparative evaluation of suitability and risk–benefit ratio for most regional techniques is provided in Table 74.1.

### Management of Nonsurgical Pain

Relief of pain associated with medical conditions such as herpes zoster, acquired immunodeficiency syndrome (AIDS), mucosal and cutaneous lesions, and cancer can be achieved using regional block techniques.<sup>24,25</sup> Children with sickle cell disease may greatly benefit from epidural analgesia during vasoocclusive crisis or thoracic syndromes with intractable pain by other means, provided that pain is localized to limited areas and concomitant fever is not due to bacteremia.<sup>26,27</sup> Epidural anesthesia and peripheral nerve blocks may enhance a physician’s multimodal pain control regimen, especially with ultrasound guidance.<sup>28</sup>

### Chronic Pain Relief and Palliative Care

Chronic pain is less unusual in children than commonly believed. Regional techniques such as epidural anesthesia, stellate ganglion blockade, or continuous peripheral nerve blocks may help treat this condition, especially in phantom limb pain and complex regional pain syndrome (CRPS), leading to pain reduction (minimal pain scores), facilitating

Virtually all regional anesthesia techniques have been reported in managing pain in children, from epidural analgesia to intrathecal infusions to celiac or brachial plexus block.<sup>32,33</sup>

### Nonanalgesic Indications

Analgesia is not the only benefit expected from the regional blockade for specific medical conditions. The sympathetic blockade is essential to protect and improve blood supply to an upper or lower extremity in the context of severe trauma.

Also, continuous epidural blockade proved effective in treating vascular insufficiency resulting from Kawasaki disease, accidental intra- arterial injection of an anesthetic drug,<sup>34</sup> penile block with a local anesthetic containing epinephrine, and severe frostbite. Peripheral nerve blocks have also successfully treated acute vascular insufficiency of the upper<sup>35</sup> and lower limb.<sup>36</sup>

In addition, regional anesthesia can be used to manage cardiac dysrhythmias, including, but not limited to, refractory ventricular tachycardia.<sup>37</sup>

### CONTRAINDICATIONS AND LIMITATIONS

### Absolute Contraindications to Peripheral Nerve Block Procedure

The only absolute medical contraindication for peripheral nerve blocks is a true allergy to local anesthetics. In children with coagulation disorders, peripheral nerve blocks



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 3 頁）

![第 74 章，原始 PDF 第 3 頁](assets/chapter-74/source-page-0003.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 4 -->

are less dangerous than neuraxial blocks.<sup>5</sup> Nevertheless, avoiding techniques that pose a risk of arterial trauma is prudent, particularly in areas where compression is difficult or impossible (infraclavicular brachial plexus block, psoas compartment block).<sup>38,39</sup> If the anticipated benefits are substantial, septicemia is not necessarily contraindicative for peripheral nerve blockade. Before performing a peripheral nerve block, careful local skin disinfection at the puncture site should be performed, especially if a catheter is implanted. Hypovolemia should ideally be corrected, but peripheral blocks are not precluded because the hemodynamic consequences are minimal.

### Absolute Contraindications to Neuraxial Blocks

Medical conditions that contraindicate neuraxial blocks in children are (1) severe coagulation disorders, which may be constitutional (hemophilia), acquired (disseminated intravascular coagulation), or therapeutic; (2) severe infection such as septicemia or meningitis; (3) intracranial tumor with increased intracranial pressure; (4) true allergy to local anesthetics (a very rare condition even with aminoesters); (5) certain chemotherapies (such as with cisplatin) prone to induce subclinical neurologic lesions that can be acutely aggravated by a block procedure; (6) uncorrected hypovolemia; and (7) cutaneous or subcutaneous lesions, whatever their nature (infection, angioma, dystrophic, tattoo) at the contemplated site of puncture. Parental refusal is a nonmedical absolute contraindication. In addition, if the child is older, assent must be obtained before performing the block.

Depending on the clinical condition of the patient and the possibility of curing (at least temporarily) the impeding disorder, a regional block may be considered despite a contraindication. After correction of hypovolemia, injection of factor VIII to a hemophilic child, or effective antibiotic treatment of a septicemic patient,<sup>40</sup> a central block may be performed, provided that the expected benefits consistently outweigh the risks by comparison with other techniques of analgesia. Also, some authors consider it acceptable to perform a caudal block in children with shunt devices under the protection of antibioprophylaxis.<sup>41</sup> A single- center study demonstrated that caudal blocks in children with ventriculoperitoneal (VP) shunts could be safely performed.<sup>42</sup>

### Other Risk Considerations

**Compartment Syndrome.** As pain is one of the primary symptoms of compartment syndrome, it is frequently claimed that any pain treatment, including regional anesthesia, is contraindicated because it can suppress this manifesting symptom, thereby delaying life- saving surgery. This refusal of pain management is neither medically nor ethically acceptable.<sup>43</sup> In the pediatric population, fractures are extremely common, whereas compartment syndromes are extremely rare; whether or not a compartment syndrome is present, severe pain is always present. The National Pediatric Epidural Audit in the United Kingdom confirmed that appropriate pain management, including continuous epidural analgesia,<sup>44</sup> does not preclude the early diagnosis.<sup>45</sup>

The European Society for Regional Anaesthesia and Pain Therapy (ESRA) and the ASRA have recently released guidelines for using regional anesthesia and compartment syndrome.<sup>5</sup>

Extreme pain is not a “manifesting” symptom of compartment syndrome but rather a late symptom. Even in university hospitals, most patients at risk are not monitored adequately. Precautions must be taken, such as avoiding closed plaster casts, elbow immobilization with an angle greater than 90 degrees,<sup>46</sup> closed reduction of supracondylar fractures of the humerus, repeat clinical evaluation of the distal perfusion and tissue oxygenation of the limb, and noninvasive monitoring of the intracompartmental pressure. If the risk is high (e.g., displaced humeral fractures, intramedullary nail fixation of tibia or radius, comatose patients), intracompartmental pressure should be monitored invasively close to the fracture site. Simple and inexpensive, the procedure requires only a venous cannula, an intravenous line, and a pressure gauge (as for central venous pressure measurement).<sup>47,48</sup>

**Hemoglobinopathies.** Children with sickle cell disease are prone to develop hemolysis in case of desaturation and undergo repeat episodes of extreme pain due to extended microthromboses when local blood flow slows down (e.g., hemoconcentration, shock, surgical tourniquet).<sup>49</sup> In the case of a danger of hypoxemia (respiratory disease) or hemodynamic disorders (surgery known to produce significant blood loss, tourniquet placement), regional (especially neuraxial) blocks should be avoided. Regional anesthesia has been shown to improve symptoms in children with sickle cell disease and decrease their pain.<sup>26</sup>

**Bone and Joint Deformities.** Minor or localized spine malformations (hemivertebra, spina bifida occulta, Scheuermann disease) do not preclude neuraxial blocks. In contrast, extensive malformations of vertebrae, spinal fusion, myelomeningoceles, open spina bifida, and major spondylolisthesis are relative contraindications.<sup>42</sup> Syndrome of the tethered cord is uncommon and frequently misdiagnosed. Suppose a cluster of hairs or a dystrophic skin lesion is present at the lower extremity of the spinous process line or in the case of minor neurologic disorders involving pelvic nerves. In that case, the diagnosis must be suspected (minimal sphincter disorders, perineal dysesthesia). This is diagnosable with ultrasound guidance. Although some authors believe this condition is not a contraindication,<sup>50</sup> it is preferable to choose an alternative analgesic technique over an epidural block. Numerous pediatric syndromes, including cerebral palsy and kyphoscoliosis, are associated with bone and joint deformities that pose more of a technical challenge than a contraindication for regional anesthesia.

**Preexisting Neurologic Disorders or Diseases.** Wellcontrolled epilepsy is not contraindicative for regional anesthesia, including neuraxial blockade. Long- considered contraindications, at least relative, are preexisting central nervous system disorders and degenerative axonal diseases, despite no data supporting the hypothesis that a regional block could worsen their course.<sup>51</sup>

### COMPLICATIONS

Regional anesthesia complications are comparable to those in adults. The current rate of complications in a large epidemiologic study was 0.12%, with age and central

<!-- PDF page 5 -->

blocks constituting the two most important risk factors.<sup>52</sup> They can be classified as local, regional, and general (or systemic).

### Local Complications

The four main types of local complications are as follows:

1.  Inappropriate needle insertion damages the nerve and surrounding anatomic structures

2.  Tissue coring and introduction of epithelial cells into tissues where they do not belong and where they can develop as compressive tumors (especially in the neuraxial area)<sup>53</sup>

3.  Injection of neurotoxic solutions (syringe mismatch, epinephrine close to a terminal artery)

4.  Leakage around the puncture site, especially when a catheter has been introduced, may cause partial block failure and favor bacterial contamination (sporadic)

These local complications are easily avoidable using adequate devices and standard precautions (appropriate dressing and bacterial precautions). Tunneling the catheter and applying a slightly compressive dressing may reduce leakage around the catheter.

Local anesthetics are also locally toxic. Myelin, adequate protection of nerve roots, is less abundant or absent in children, potentially making the nerves more sensitive to local anesthetics. In animals, it has been demonstrated that the sensitivity of nerve fibers to local anesthetics is inversely correlated with age.<sup>54</sup> However, in most cases, perineural injection is around the muscle. The myotoxicity of local anesthetics has been previously demonstrated in humans and animals,<sup>55</sup> primarily through mitochondrial damage. This also has been shown in young animals.<sup>56</sup> By comparing continuous bupivacaine perineural administration in adult and young rats, the authors showed that muscle, mitochondrial, and ultrastructural toxicity was significantly greater in the juvenile animal group,<sup>56</sup> thus emphasizing the need to use lower dosages and greater caution in younger patients.

### Systemic Complications

Systemic complications usually result from an accidental intravenous injection of local anesthetics or, less frequently, excessive dosing.<sup>57,58</sup> Systemic toxicity is essential of two types – neurologic and cardiac – both caused by blocking sodium and potassium channels. Early signs of neurologic toxicity (tinnitus, malaise, metallic taste in the mouth) are unfortunately masked by general anesthesia. The main complications are heart conduction disorders, cardiac arrhythmias (bradycardia or tachycardia), and atrioventricular block. QRS widening, bradycardia, and torsades de pointes are followed by ventricular fibrillation, asystole, or both.<sup>59</sup> Nevertheless, signs of cardiac and neurologic toxicity occur at lower plasma concentrations of bupivacaine than ropivacaine.<sup>60</sup> This toxicity may be aggravated by a decrease in plasma binding protein, mainly α1-acid glycoprotein (AGP), resulting in a more significant proportion of the unbound form of local anesthetic. The plasma concentration of this protein is low at birth and tends to increase with the child’s age to reach values equivalent to those of adults at ten months.<sup>61</sup> However, special care must be taken during continuous injections; the dosage of local anesthetic

should be systematically reduced in very young children or after prolonged administration (>48 hours).

Systemic complications can be life- threatening and should be managed in the same way as adults. The significant difference between adults and children is that neurologic signs do not precede cardiovascular complications but are concomitant with cerebral toxicity.<sup>62</sup> In addition to pharmacokinetic factors, the rapid heart rate of children may increase the risk for cardiac toxicity induced by local anesthetic toxicity. Even if toxic events occur with ropivacaine, small doses of epinephrine should produce rapid recovery. Impaired ventricular conduction is the primary manifestation of local anesthetic toxicity. Treatment includes oxygenation, cardiac massage, and epinephrine, given in small incremental boluses beginning with 1 to 2 mcg/kg.<sup>63</sup> If ventricular fibrillation persists, defibrillation (2–4 J/kg) is performed. Although resuscitation measures must be initiated immediately, the specific treatment of local anesthetic toxicity is rapid administration of Intralipid.<sup>63</sup> The recommended dose of 20% Intralipid for pediatric patients is 2 to 5 mL/kg by intravenous bolus. If the cardiac function does not return, this dose (up to 10 mL/kg **)** is repeated.<sup>64</sup>

### Epidemiology

Available pediatric information is limited. The first report of the American Society of Anesthesiologists (ASA) closed claims analysis contained 238 pediatric cases (10% of claims), but only seven involved children who received regional anesthesia<sup>65</sup> ; however, at that time, regional anesthesia was not commonly used in children, making this low rate of complications meaningless. In 1996, the 1- year prospective study of the French- Language Society of Pediatric Anesthesiologists evaluated 85,412 pediatric anesthetic procedures, including 24,409 involving regional anesthesia.<sup>66</sup> Twenty- three complications (no sequelae, no death, and no legal consequences) were found, all following neuraxial blocks. In 2000, the Australian Incident Monitoring Study<sup>67</sup> included 2000 claims involving 160 pediatric cases with a regional block procedure (83 epidurals, 42 spinals, 14 brachial plexus, 4 Bier blocks, three ophthalmic blocks, and 14 local infiltrations). The most significant single cause of complications was circulatory problems; 24 drug errors (including 10 “wrong drugs” and 14 “inappropriate use”) were found. In 2007, the British National Pediatric Epidural Audit<sup>45</sup> reported 96 incidents in 10,633 epidural blocks performed, as follows:

- Fifty- six (0.53%) were associated with inserting or maintaining epidural anesthesia, and most were of low severity; only one child had residual effects from cauda equina syndrome (after a programming error of the infusion pump).

- Forty (0.38%), mainly pressure sores,<sup>45</sup> were believed to be associated with the epidural infusion technique.

Twenty- eight infection- related incidents occurred, of which 85% were relatively minor skin infections; caudal catheters did not increase the incidence of infections. A significantly higher rate of incidents occurred in the neonatal age group, mainly because of drug errors (13 cases) and local anesthetic toxicity (1 case) unrelated to catheter insertion. Six children older than eight years of age had mild postdural

<!-- PDF page 6 -->

puncture headaches. Four patients developed compartment syndrome, but the epidural infusion did not mask the condition.

From November 2005 to October 2006, a large epidemiologic study recorded the characteristics and developments of regional anesthesia in children in 47 French hospitals.<sup>52</sup> As previously demonstrated by Rochette and colleagues,<sup>68</sup> the authors reported a radical change in regional practice among French anesthesiologists with a transition from using predominantly neuraxial blocks to peripheral nerve blocks, including catheter techniques. A recent 1- year prospective survey of regional anesthesia evaluated complications and side effects in 31,132 cases of regional anesthesia. Complications (41, involving 40 patients) were rare, usually minor, and did not result in sequelae. The study recorded a very low overall rate of complications of 0.12%, significantly six times lower for peripheral blocks than for neuraxial blocks. Age was also a risk factor because complications were higher before six months of life than after (0.4% before vs. 0.1% after six months). Fifteen cases of cardiotoxicity were observed, of which 87% occurred with a central block. The occurrence of complications does not increase with the use of catheters.

An extensive North American regional anesthesia registry (Pediatric Regional Anesthesia Network [PRAN]) has published prospective data on 100,000 blocks in over 20 centers. The incidence of local anesthetic systemic toxicity (LAST) was 0.76:10,000 cases, most occurring in infants. No permanent neurologic deficits were reported; however, the risk of transient neurologic deficit was 2.4:10,000 and was not different between peripheral and neuraxial blocks.<sup>69</sup>

In conclusion, regional block procedures – mainly neuraxial blocks – are associated with adverse incidents (∼0.5%) that are mostly minor but occasionally severe. Most of these complications result from insufficient precautions during the block procedure (drug errors) and postoperatively (pressure sores). Also of significant importance is that compartment syndrome is not hidden by regional anesthesia, provided that adequate monitoring is guaranteed.<sup>5</sup>

## Safety, Precautions, and Discharge Criteria

### GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS

Infection, hematoma, and local anesthetic toxicity are uncommon in children undergoing regional anesthesia. Still, the ASRA/ESRA joint committee has recently proposed a practice advisory to prevent and treat these complications. The aseptic technique prevents infection. Povidone–iodine is inferior to chlorhexidine for pre- block skin preparation. A tunneled catheter is preferred using the caudal route or leaving the epidural catheter in place for more than three days. Postoperative care requires daily inspection of the epidural catheter insertion site. Neuraxial and deep peripheral nerve blocks are contraindicated if coagulation tests are abnormal. Heparin thromboprophylaxis with a safety interval of two half- lives plus the time required for heparin to reach maximal levels balances bleeding risk and thrombosis risk when removing epidural catheters in low- molecularweight heparin (LMWH)- treated patients.

Peripheral nerve blocks guided by ultrasound reduce vascular puncture and local anesthetic toxicity. Aspiration before injection decreases systemic local toxicity.<sup>70</sup> Clinicians should proceed cautiously when using local anesthetics, even though systemic toxicity is rare in infants. It is essential to pay extra attention to infants and newborns because of the unique physiologic challenges they face.<sup>1</sup>

### OPTIMAL LOCATION FOR PERFORMING REGIONAL BLOCK

Due to the anesthetic nature of regional techniques, they must be performed in fully equipped operating rooms with access to all necessary monitoring, anesthetic, and resuscitation equipment and drugs in case of emergency. In addition, the anesthesiologist needs support from nurses and technicians who are trained to keep patients safe in an emergency and capable of providing adequate monitoring. A mature adolescent may consent to have a block done in the preoperative area, but otherwise, blocks should be typically performed in the operating room.

### SEDATION AND GENERAL ANESTHESIA

Regional procedures are performed on adult patients awake, with or without sedation, but not typically under general anesthesia. The same treatment can be offered to calm pediatric patients and is sometimes requested by the child.<sup>71</sup> Most children must be unconscious during the block procedure. If general anesthesia is not medically contraindicated, it is generally accepted that regional blocks may be performed with the patient under light general anesthesia; large databases have demonstrated the safety of this procedure.<sup>14,52,69,72</sup>

### PATIENT MONITORING AND SAFETY PRECAUTIONS DURING THE BLOCK PROCEDURE

### Monitoring and Anesthesia Chart

Even if general anesthesia is not used and the surgical procedure is primarily based on a regional technique, the anesthesiologist should always apply routine intraoperative monitoring, including electrocardiographic (ECG) tracings, blood pressure, and temperature, respiratory rate, and oxygen saturation measurements. In addition, an intravenous line must be established before any injection of local anesthetic,<sup>73</sup> and vital parameters, techniques, and doses of local anesthetics must be reported on a detailed anesthesia chart. It is also imperative to site mark the area to be blocked and get consent if under ten years, and consent and assent from the patient if they are older.

### Technique of Injection

Techniques in adults and children are similar. Of prime importance is to evaluate the effects on ECG tracings of a test dose containing epinephrine (0.1 mL/kg up to 3 mL containing 0.5–1 mcg/kg of epinephrine) for 30 to 60 seconds. Any elevation of ST segment or increase in T- wave amplitude,<sup>74–76</sup> followed by an increase in blood pressure but only occasionally by tachycardia, suggests inadvertent intravenous injection and necessitates cessation of the

<!-- PDF page 7 -->

procedure; when epinephrine is contraindicated, isoproterenol 0.05 to 0.1 mcg/kg should be used instead.<sup>77</sup>

### Assessment of the Block

After every block procedure, the quality and extension of analgesia must be evaluated before surgical incision. However, this evaluation is challenging, even in conscious children. Gentle skin pinching is the most dependable technique of sensory testing, especially in lightly anesthetized children. One method is to use ice in a plastic bag to determine the efficacy of the block in lightly sedated children; however, it may be difficult to elicit any response in children under general anesthesia. Electrical stimulation using a nerve stimulator at different threshold intensities proved suitable in healthy volunteers, but data on children are limited. Skin temperature is not suitable, and whether pupillary reflex dilation of 0.2 mm is sensitive to the loss of analgesia is not clinically useful.<sup>78</sup>

### Postoperative Monitoring in the Recovery Room

After combined regional and general anesthesia, all children must be transferred to a recovery room (postanesthesia care unit [PACU]), where respiratory and hemodynamic status stability is checked like after general anesthesia. In addition to this standard postanesthetic care, they require repeat evaluations of the anesthetized area. In the case of motor blockade, which should be avoided as often as possible, it is essential to verify that its distribution corresponds to the area supplied to the blocked nerve. Patient positioning must be carefully and regularly checked to avoid pressure points. The possibility that a compartment syndrome is evolving must always be kept in mind, and both the hemodynamic status of the relevant limb and the quality of analgesia must be repeatedly evaluated.

Urinary retention can be of concern after neuraxial blocks. In many institutions, however, voiding is not requested before discharging the child. As a result, adult surgical patients under regional blockade often bypass the postanesthesia recovery room. Even if no sedative has been given to children, it is not wise to do so, and adequate monitoring and skilled assistance improve recovery from immediate postoperative incidents.<sup>79</sup> After short procedures, sudden cessation of external stimulation may unmask some compensated adverse effects (hemodynamic and respiratory especially) that are not harmful if detected early. As noted earlier, ropivacaine and, to a lesser extent, levobupivacaine, have a longer Tmax (up to 2 hours) and Cmax in infants; therefore, after short- duration surgeries, these patients may leave the operating room before local anesthetic plasma peak concentration has been reached. Monitoring them in a PACU up to 2 hours after the block procedure is recommended.

### DISCHARGE CRITERIA AFTER SINGLE- SHOT PROCEDURES

The same criteria apply with discharge from the PACU as do after general anesthesia (pediatric adaptation of Aldrete score or specific scoring method in use in the relevant institution); in the absence of motor blockade, children usually may be discharged within 30 minutes. In other cases, discharge depends on the behavior of the child. Partial

restoration of motor function is mandatory, even in quiet children with caring families. Boisterous children should not leave before motor functions have been fully restored; additionally, protective dressings (including casts) may help prevent harm to the operated limb. The persistence of sensory blockade is only a contraindication to early discharge if the familial environment is inadequate. Pain medication should be systematically prescribed and administered regularly to prevent the return of intense pain at home when the sensory block is no longer effective.<sup>80</sup> Most additives do not preclude early discharge except for epidural or intrathecal opioids, especially morphine or hydromorphone, necessitating the child to stay hospitalized overnight.

### MANAGEMENT OF CONTINUOUS REGIONAL TECHNIQUES

Children with parent- controlled or continuous epidural infusion should remain hospitalized to be adequately monitored. Occasionally, a few selected patients can be allowed to return home with an epidural catheter, mainly in the context of chronic pain or cancer pain in terminally ill children. These continuous techniques are newer in pediatrics<sup>22</sup> and not yet widely used. Studies on peripheral nerve blocks in children monitored at home have reported a low complication rate and good- quality analgesia.<sup>81,82</sup> In one institution, children suffering from CRPS were treated with peripheral nerve catheters at home.<sup>29</sup> The use of an elastomeric disposable device and electronic pump with intermittent bolus to deliver local anesthetic allows for simplifying the management of children at home and a nursing economy. Such management will probably gain wider acceptance among teenagers, but it should be carefully considered only after a thorough evaluation of small children.

## Relevant Differences Between Children and Adults

### ANATOMIC CONSIDERATIONS

Regional anesthesia safety depends on anatomy knowledge. “A thorough knowledge of descriptive and topographic anatomy with regards to nerve distribution is a condition which anyone desirous of attempting to study regional anesthesia should fulfill,” wrote Dr. Gaston Labat in his 1924 book, _Regional Anesthesia: Its Technique and Clinical Application_ .<sup>83</sup> Therefore, effectively matching regional anesthesia to surgical needs requires knowledge of the anatomic development and its relationship to nerve and its innervation.

### Growth- Induced Body Size Change

One of the most apparent differences between children and adults is their relative weight and size. After 10–15 years of growth, “normal” full- term neonates will have increased their weight by more than 12 (>1200%) and their height by more than 3 (>300%) from their birth weight of 3–3.5 kg and 50 cm, respectively.

In the embryonic stage, the spinal cord fills the spinal canal; however, as the vertebrae develop, they outgrow the cord, the remainder of the spinal nerves, and their

<!-- PDF page 8 -->

protective neural coverings.<sup>84</sup> At birth, the cord (conus medullaris) terminates at the L3 or L4 level, and the dura mater ends at the S3 or S4 sacral vertebra level. Only at the end of the first year of life is the adult level attained – L1 for the conus medullaris and S2 for the dural sac (Fig. 74.1). Throughout infancy and childhood, anatomic relationships and landmarks are constantly changing as a child grows. This can make it challenging to perform regional procedures and requires a working knowledge of developmental anatomy and the help of accurate techniques for finding anatomic spaces and nerve trunks. In the pediatric population, congenital malformations, genetic disorders, and the effects of fetal and neonatal asphyxia (cerebral palsy) often require frequent surgery to help the child ambulate or adapt to everyday childhood life. Table 74.2 lists the main anatomic and physiologic factors in children that can have implications for performing regional block procedures.

### Delayed Bone Ossification and Sacral Vertebrae Fusion

Neonatal bones, particularly the vertebrae, are primarily cartilaginous. Because cartilage offers low resistance to penetration, sharp needles can easily penetrate them, and ossification nuclei can be badly injured, impeding future bone and joint formation. Therefore, bone contact should be avoided wherever feasible while performing nerve block, particularly in newborns. However, its cartilaginous structure facilitates ultrasonic and fluoroscopic penetration in regional block guiding.

### Spine Curvature Development

In addition to the absence of osteophytes, spinal flexibility can be a substantial advantage for performing ultrasoundguided pediatric neuraxial block. A single spinal curvature is present at birth and can be easily counteracted by forced flexion practically throughout childhood due to constant flexibility. Regardless of the intervertebral level,


![](assets/chapter-74/74._Regional_Anesthesia_in_Children.pdf-0008-07.png)


Shoulders square<br>to the bed<br>Conus medullaris<br>(>1 year)<br>Conus medullaris<br>(Infant)<br>lntercristal<br>line (>1 year)<br>lntercristal<br>line (infant)<br>

**Fig. 74.1 Spinal development and its surface landmarks.** Positions of the conus medullaris and intercristal line for infants younger than one year old ( _red_ ) and children older than one year old ( _blue_ ) are depicted.

the orientation of epidural needles can remain similar throughout.

### Loose Fasciae and Epidural Fat

The fascia and the perivascular sleeve are only tenuously attached to the underlying structures (e.g., nerves, muscles, tendons, and vessels). This allows for the widespread distribution of local anesthetics, which can lead to effective nerve blockades across all techniques but can also spread to nerves and anatomic spaces that are not intended. In newborns and young children, the epidural fat is pliable (up to 6–7 years of age). Because of the epidural space’s fluidity and the spinal cord’s loose attachment to the sheaths surrounding it, a relatively large volume of epidural local anesthetics (up to 1.25 mL/kg) is required to achieve desired levels of anesthesia.

### Delayed Nerve Fiber Myelination

Cervical neuromeres are the first to undergo myelination during development, and this process then spreads cephalad and caudad.<sup>85,86</sup> However, myelination is only complete after the age of twelve. Infants have particularly poor myelination; the incomplete development of their nerve fibers is the primary cause of their inability to walk. The increased ability of local anesthetics to reach and block nerve fibers is also a significant pharmacologic consequence of this condition. Thus, the effectiveness of nerve blockade from diluted local anesthetic solutions is equivalent to that of solutions at least twice as concentrated in adults. On the other hand, infants experience a shorter blockade due to local anesthetics and are less likely to become trapped within myelin and released gradually. Increased local circulation also leads to more rapid and significant vascular absorption.

### PAIN PERCEPTION CONSIDERATIONS

Injury or disease affecting the somatosensory nervous system leads to pain. However, pain perception must be viewed as consisting of three components that interact<sup>87–89</sup> : sensory–discriminative, cognitive–evaluative, and motivational–affective. The perception of pain’s intensity, location, quality, and behavior is sensory–discriminative. The cognitive component pertains to thoughts about the situation that are influenced by past experiences and knowledge. In contrast, the motivational–affective part relates to emotional responses such as anger, anxiety, and fear that motivate the reaction to pain dimensions. All aspects interact to generate altered physiologic outputs and, eventually, altered behavior.

Physiologically, unmyelinated C fibers are primarily responsible for transmitting pain (“slow” pain or “real” pain). Pain causes protective reflexes, including autonomic responses, muscular contraction, and rigidity. C fibers are fully functional from early fetal life onward. While connections between C fibers and dorsal horn neurons are not mature before the second week of postnatal life, C fiber–transmitted nociceptive stimulations to the dorsal horn cause long- lasting responses.<sup>90,91</sup> This is likely due to extensive neuronal depolarization caused by substance P production. During the first two weeks of life, neonates’ exaggerated response to nociceptive stimulation

decreases as substance P dorsal horn receptors decrease. Birth- immature inhibitory control pathways also develop simultaneously.

Depending on the stage of the infant (full- term vs. preterm) and the infant’s cumulative experience with pain, painful procedures during the neonatal period modify subsequent pain responses in infancy and childhood.<sup>92</sup> Full- term neonates respond to noxious stimulations with

heightened behavioral responsiveness, whereas preterm neonates respond with a dampened response. When sufficient analgesic drugs (local anesthetics or opioids) are administered before painful procedures, infants exhibit less procedural pain and a decrease in the magnitude of longterm changes in pain behaviors.<sup>92</sup> However, the inability of young patients to communicate with their caregivers and precisely express their distress and discomfort poses a



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 9 頁）

![第 74 章，原始 PDF 第 9 頁](assets/chapter-74/source-page-0009.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 10 -->

significant challenge in assessing and, at times, even identifying pain in children for titrating adequate analgesia. Although pediatric pain has received considerable attention over the past two decades, reliable age- related pain scales have been challenging to develop in accurately assessing the severity of pain and the efficacy of its treatment.

### PHYSIOLOGIC CONSIDERATIONS

Surgery generates a neuroendocrine stress response in neonates, infants, and children,<sup>89,93</sup> resulting in undesirable alterations of the metabolic state and immune function.<sup>94</sup> Epidural anesthesia diminishes or even suppresses this stress response.<sup>95–97</sup> Central blocks do not affect the left ventricular function and are virtually free of measurable hemodynamic effects, at least up to the age of 8 years.<sup>98,99</sup> Epidural anesthesia does not result in systemic or pulmonary hemodynamic changes as measured by mean blood pressure, the end- diastolic diameter of the left ventricle, ejection fraction of the left ventricle, and mean velocity circumferential fiber shortening.<sup>100</sup> However, the pulmonary Doppler flow velocity is decreased during epidural anesthesia, probably due to increased pulmonary arterial resistance. Therefore, preloading with saline is not recommended in children, and even in adolescents, fluid therapy or injection of vasoactive agents is rarely required.

### PSYCHOLOGICAL CONSIDERATIONS

Children are frightened by new environmental conditions in the operating room, and most of them cannot cope with their anxiety.<sup>101,102</sup> They feel abandoned by their parents and exposed to strangers threatening them with needles. Furthermore, children younger than ten have not acquired their complete body image and cannot distinguish between adjacent parts of their body, such as the forearm and arm. Young patients cannot understand the concepts of paresthesia and differential blockade (“touch” is not “pain”). Thus, localization of nerve trunks and anatomic spaces requires physical methods independent of the patient’s cooperation (loss of resistance [LOR] seeking, nerve stimulation, ultrasound techniques). Infants and most children cannot cope with their anxiety and fear of needles; therefore, deep sedation or general anesthesia may be needed before attempting a block procedure to avoid panic attacks and unwanted movements. Prospective regional anesthesia registries have demonstrated that awake versus asleep placement of regional blocks in children has no deleterious effects on outcomes.<sup>6</sup>

Regional anesthesia has a significant psychological impact. A pain- free postoperative course improves the morale of the patient, the family, and the nurses. Surgeons are happy to examine quiet, alert, and manageable patients. Occasionally, adverse psychological effects can be observed: persistent motor (even sensory) blocks postoperatively may be frightening to some children (especially those 3–5 years of age) and their parents even though precise explanations had been given preoperatively concerning the expected course of such events during the postoperative period. Offering social, environmental conditions, empathy, and additional explanations on local anesthetic pharmacology can reduce this postoperative anxiety.

## Pharmacology of Local Anesthetics and Additives

Children’s pharmacologic properties are primarily influenced by (1) the immaturity of certain enzyme pathways and their replacement by other biochemical pathways and (2) the progressive increase in body surface area concurrent with the growth process. Dosing for drugs based on body surface area is the same as (or a simple ratio of) adult dosing.<sup>103</sup> However, body surface area is difficult to measure. In practice, doses are calculated based on the child’s body weight and must be constantly adjusted as the child grows; dosage errors are not infrequent.

### LOCAL ANESTHETICS

Chemical properties and mechanisms of action of local anesthetics are described elsewhere in this book (see also Chapter 25); they are similar during childhood, and only pharmacokinetic properties, particularly in neonates and infants, may differ significantly.<sup>58</sup>

### Local Fixation

In contrast to adults, local anesthetic fixation is decreased and spread is increased in infants, particularly within the epidural space, because epidural fat is more fluid and less densely packed. The primary effects are (1) a quicker onset of action, (2) a wider longitudinal and circumferential distribution of local anesthetics, and (3) a shorter duration of action due to a decrease in secondary release from local binding sites.

### Diffusion and Dispersion to Target Neural Tissue

Local anesthetic action targets sodium channels within nerve fibers that are voltage dependent. Therefore, nonionized molecules can only penetrate biologic membranes, and the penetration rate depends on the number and (increasing with age) thickness of sheaths.

### Systemic Absorption and Distribution

**plasma protein binding.** Nonionized local anesthetics traverse the capillary wall near the injection site with relative ease. Because cardiac output and local blood flow are two to three times higher in infants than adults, systemic local anesthetic absorption is proportionally increased, and vasoactive agents such as epinephrine are highly effective at reducing systemic uptake.

Local anesthetics undergo plasma protein binding primarily to human serum albumin (HSA) and α1- acid glycoprotein (AGP) or orosomucoid once they have penetrated the vascular bed. Local anesthetics have a low affinity for HSA, and many pharmacologic agents can compete for available binding sites. In addition, plasma levels of HSA are low during the first months of life, particularly in preterm and fasted infants; consequently, the protection offered by HSA against the systemic toxicity of local anesthetics is low and declines postoperatively. The affinity of AGP for local anesthetics is 5000 to 10,000 times greater than that of HSA, making AGP a highly effective systemic toxicity protector (which depends on the unbound free form of local anesthetics). However, the plasma concentration of AGP is

<!-- PDF page 11 -->

inadequate at birth (0.2–0.3 g/L) and does not reach adult levels (0.7–1.0 g/L) until after one year of age.<sup>104–106</sup>

As the plasma concentration of the two proteins able to bind local anesthetics is low at birth, the free fraction of all local anesthetics is increased in infants; consequently, the maximum doses of all aminoamides must be significantly reduced in this age group.<sup>106</sup> Except in the case of liver insufficiency, AGP plasma levels may rise due to surgical stress, especially in infants undergoing infection or emergency surgery.<sup>104</sup> The increase in plasma AGP concentration can alter the proportion of ropivacaine’s free fraction, thereby increasing the plasma concentration of this molecule’s bound fraction and protecting against the systemic toxicity of local anesthetics.<sup>107</sup> By keeping the plasma concentration of local anesthetics within the safety margins, these phenomena can drastically reduce the potential risk associated with a single injection.

**red cell storage.** Local anesthetics distribute to red blood cells, which retain 20 to 30 percent of the total dose, depending on the anesthetic and hematocrit. Therefore, red cell storage typically has a minor effect on the pharmacokinetics of local anesthetics, except in the following instances:

- In neonates, high hematocrit values (which may exceed 70%) and erythrocyte enlargement (physiologic macrocytosis) result in consistent “entrapment” of local anesthetics, thereby lowering peak plasma concentration (Cmax) values after a single injection but increasing secondary release, thereby extending the half- life of all local anesthetics.

- In infants, physiologic anemia reduces red cell storage and its protective effect against systemic toxicity of local anesthetics (only after a single- shot injection) when plasma protein binding sites are saturated – that is, when blood concentrations are close to toxic levels.

**absorption from peripheral sites.** Absorption of local anesthetics deposited along mucous membranes is increased in infants.<sup>64</sup> Mucosal topical anesthesia has long been considered contraindicated in this age group. However, the technique can be safely used with certain precautions: selecting specific transmucosal patches<sup>108</sup> or sprays with diluted lidocaine<sup>109,110</sup> and recognizing that topical lidocaine exaggerates laryngomalacia.<sup>111</sup>

After cutaneous application of EMLA (lidocaine and prilocaine) cream, peak plasma concentrations occur 4 hours later and remain low- field<sup>112</sup> – less than 200 ng/mL for lidocaine and less than 131 ng/mL for prilocaine, even in infants younger than six months of age.

Absorption of local anesthetics from compartment blocks (e.g., fascia iliaca, umbilical, ilioinguinal, and pudendal blocks) follows the same biphasic curve for the epidural space.<sup>113–116</sup> Because of the extended absorption surface, injection of highly concentrated solutions of local anesthetics often leads to high, occasionally potentially toxic peak plasma concentrations, especially with 0.5% ropivacaine,<sup>116</sup> whereas using more diluted solutions results in relatively low plasma concentrations.

Absorption from peripheral nerve conduction blocks also follows a similar biphasic curve with different Cmax and Tmax values depending on the local anesthetic, the addition of

epinephrine, and the injection site; the more distal the injection, the slower the absorption process (as in adults).

Ultrasound- guided interfascial plane blocks, a new type of regional technique, are increasingly common (see Truncal Blocks, below). Most experts believe that increasing the volume of local anesthetic used for an interfascial plane block is vital for adequate analgesia. However, there is serious concern about local anesthetic toxicity in young patients when using large volumes of local anesthetic. It can be challenging to provide adequate volumes of local anesthetic through bilateral catheters in patients who are neonates, are underweight, or have organ dysfunction due to the local anesthetic’s potential for systemic toxicity (LAST). In addition, pharmacokinetic information on intermittent fascial plane blocks remains scant.<sup>117–119</sup> This information is essential for minimizing systemic toxicity when treating pediatric patients. While it is unclear how much of an intermittent bolus is absorbed systemically from fascial plane blocks at a steady state, similar absorption may occur from different injection sites along the fascial plane because of the plane’s large surface area for local anesthetic spread and diffusion.<sup>120</sup> Nonetheless, it is essential to emphasize that dosing must be individualized and closely monitored.

**absorption from the epidural space.** Epidural space absorption of local anesthetics has been extensively studied. The same absorption kinetics are reported in children and infants, but the biphasic shape of the plasma concentration curve is less pronounced in younger patients. The peak plasma concentration and slope of the decreasing concentration curve are increased,<sup>121</sup> while the time (Tmax) to reach Cmax remains unchanged; for instance, the Tmax of bupivacaine is approximately 30 minutes regardless of the patient’s age.

The notable exception is ropivacaine. Tmax is prolonged in infants after caudal or lumbar epidural injection by up to two hours, and Cmax rises.<sup>122</sup> Infant surgeries are usually brief, so this significant increase in Cmax and Tmax values must be considered and monitored. Before peak plasma concentration, the children may have left the operating room and PACU less than two hours after the caudal or epidural procedure. This unique pharmacokinetic profile may be due to enzyme immaturity, slower systemic uptake, and lower distribution volume.<sup>123,124</sup> Intrinsic vasoconstrictive properties of ropivacaine also may play a role in the same way as the addition of epinephrine.

Importantly, levobupivacaine displays a similar pharmacokinetic profile. After a caudal injection of levobupivacaine 2 mg/kg in infants younger than two years of age, the Cmax range is 0.41 to 2.42 mcg/mL (0.91 ± 0.40 mcg/mL), which is higher than the Cmax reported after caudal injection of the same dose of racemic bupivacaine.<sup>125</sup> Tmax values are also delayed (50 vs. 30 minutes) in infants younger than three months due to reduced plasma clearance.<sup>126</sup>

When repeat injections are considered, the epidural dose must be reduced to keep Cmax values in the same range as that resulting from the first injection. For the second injection, the following recommendations can be made: reduce the dose to one- third of the initial amount and do not inject it less than 30 minutes (lidocaine, mepivacaine, prilocaine) or 45 minutes (bupivacaine, levobupivacaine, ropivacaine) after the first injection; or inject half of the initial dose, but 60 minutes (lidocaine, mepivacaine, prilocaine,

<!-- PDF page 12 -->

chloroprocaine) or 90 minutes (bupivacaine, levobupivacaine, ropivacaine) after the first injection.

If repeated injections are necessary, dosing should be further reduced to half of the second dose (i.e., one- sixth of the initial amount) while respecting the same delay as the second injection.

Continuous infusions aim to produce a steady- state concentration at the 24th hour postoperatively. This goal is easily achieved in adolescents with infusion rates of approximately 0.3 mg/kg/h of bupivacaine and levobupivacaine or 0.4 mg/kg/h of ropivacaine.

In infants, infusion rates must be reduced,<sup>127,128</sup> not exceeding 0.2 mg/kg/h with bupivacaine (or equipotent doses of other local anesthetics) in infants younger than four months and 0.25 mg/kg/h in older infants. Infants younger than four months (occasionally up to 9 months) may develop systemic toxicity even at these “safe” infusion rates with racemic bupivacaine because no steady- state plasma concentration is reached, even at 48 hours. In this age group, levobupivacaine<sup>108</sup> or ropivacaine<sup>109</sup> instead of racemic bupivacaine is preferred because stable plateau concentrations are obtained from the 24th hour onward.

**pulmonary extraction.** Aminoamides enter the right cardiac cavities and then the pulmonary circulation, where they undergo plasma protein linkage and erythrocyte storage before being extracted by the lung. Their plasma concentration consistently drops in the pulmonary veins and systemic arterial circulation (primarily the coronary and

cerebral arteries). Therefore, pulmonary extraction serves as a transient defense against systemic toxicity. But some medical conditions block this defense mechanism. Some drugs, like propranolol, reduce pulmonary extraction in a clinically significant way. Additionally, due to pulmonary circulation bypass, children with right- to- left shunts experience significant increases in the arterial plasma concentration of local anesthetics; even with low doses, they are susceptible to systemic toxicity.<sup>129</sup>

**distribution volume.** All aminoamides have a volume distribution at the steady state (Vdss) of 1 to 2 L/kg following intravenous injection (Table 74.3). However, due to the “flip- flop” effect, calculated distribution is frequently significantly increased following administration at other sites, especially for long- lasting local anesthetics. The distribution volume of all local anesthetics is more significant in infants and newborns than in adults due to higher extracellular fluid content (Table 74.4), which has two effects: (1) a significant reduction in peak plasma concentration of all local anesthetics, reducing the risk of systemic toxicity after a single dose; and (2) accumulation with reinjections, which lengthens the drug’s half- life and plasma concentration while concurrently increasing its elimination half- life.

**hepatic extraction and clearance of aminoamides.** Short- acting local anesthetics undergo a high level of hepatic extraction (0.65 to 0.75 ratio for lidocaine), primarily dependent on hepatic blood flow and only

<!-- PDF page 13 -->

minimally on plasma concentration. Only limited pediatric data are available for levobupivacaine. During the first months of life, the clearance of levobupivacaine increases after a single injection; however, during continuous infusion (even with 0.0625% levobupivacaine), it tends to decrease to almost the same extent as that of racemic bupivacaine, and plasma concentrations do not reach a plateau.<sup>126</sup>

**placental transfer.** Placental extraction may systematically impact the distribution of local anesthetics in the tissue in pregnant women. The placental transfer is influenced by protein binding. For lidocaine, prilocaine, and bupivacaine, the concentration ratio between umbilical venous blood and maternal arterial blood is roughly 0.73, 0.85, and 0.32, respectively. The placental transfer of d- bupivacaine is greater than that of l- bupivacaine, but only when solutions containing epinephrine are used, suggesting that chirality may also be a factor, at least for bupivacaine.<sup>130</sup> The placental transfer of most aminoesters is not a problem because of the quick plasma hydrolysis that occurs. Tetracaine and cocaine, which have slow hydrolysis, are only employed for topical treatments or (for tetracaine only) spinal anesthesia; because their systemic uptake is slow and their plasma concentrations stay very low, they do not provide a threat to placental transfer.

**metabolism.** Procaine and chloroprocaine are partially metabolized by hepatic cholinesterase. Cholinesterases in plasma rapidly hydrolyze aminoesters. This enzyme activity is low at birth (although no negative clinical effects are anticipated) and gradually reaches adult levels by one year of age.<sup>131</sup> Chloroprocaine is eliminated at the fastest rate (4.7 mol/mL/h), procaine at a slower rate (1.1 mol/mL/h), and cocaine at only 0.3 mol/mL/h.

Aminoamides are metabolized within the liver and subjected to two enzymatic reactions. Phase I reactions occur first, during which oxidation of the amide link is achieved within hepatic microsomes by the cytochrome P (CYP)450 enzyme superfamily. Phase II reactions, during which glucuronic acid or amino acid residues are appended to phase I metabolites, produce atoxic and water- soluble compounds, which are thus easily eliminated from the body.

CYP450 enzymatic activities are reduced during the first months of life. CYP3A4 mainly metabolizes bupivacaine in adults, but this enzyme is defective in infants. However, fetal CYP3A7 remains very active in infants, thus allowing the metabolism of bupivacaine to be almost as effective as with CYP3A4.<sup>64</sup> Ropivacaine and levobupivacaine<sup>126</sup> are metabolized by CYP1A2, which is not fully functional before the third year, and, to a minor extent, by CYP3A4. This enzyme immaturity is clinically relevant but with little consequences (lower clearance, delayed Tmax, and, for ropivacaine only, increased Cmax but within clinically acceptable levels): it does not preclude administering these local anesthetics in neonates and infants.

Phase II reactions, especially glucuronic–Vdss conjugation, are immature at birth and remain until the third year of life. However, other conjugation pathways, such as sulfoconjugation, are active and effective during the first months of life.

**elimination half- life.** Elimination half- life (t½β) depends on both distribution and metabolism. It can be calculated

using the formula below (Cp is the plasma clearance, and Vdss the distribution volume at the steady state):


![](assets/chapter-74/74._Regional_Anesthesia_in_Children.pdf-0013-09.png)


t½  (0.693  Vdss )/Cp<br>

t½β is the same in children older than one year of age and adults, mainly because the increase in Vdss is compensated by a concomitant rise in Cp (related in part to higher hepatic blood flow in children, whose liver accounts for 4% of body weight, vs. only 2% in adults). Before one year, Cp is low, and the elimination half- life of all local anesthetics is prolonged (see Table 74.3), thus favoring accumulation with repeat injections. Nevertheless, Bricker and colleagues<sup>132</sup> measured no consistent differences in pharmacokinetic parameters between infants and adults.

**systemic toxicity.** Clinical manifestations of neurotoxicity have been documented at plasma concentrations between 7 and 10 mcg/mL for lidocaine or mepivacaine and 1.5 to 2 mcg/mL (intraoperatively) to 2 to 2.5 mcg/ mL (postoperatively) for bupivacaine. Nonetheless, plasma concentrations of bupivacaine greater than 4 mcg/mL have been documented without clinical harm. Based on research with adult volunteers, the following toxicity levels for the unbound form of local anesthetics have been determined:

ᑏ 0.3 mcg/mL for unbound bupivacaine

ᑏ 0.6 mcg/mL for unbound levobupivacaine or ropivacaine

Because plasma protein binding is lower in infants, hazards of systemic toxicity might be increased, as cardiac toxicity is usually concomitant with, not preceded by, central nervous system toxicity.

### ADJUVANTS

Opioids as adjuvants are applied primarily in neuraxial sites and not in peripheral sites. The elimination half- life of neuraxial opioids is considerably increased in neonates and infants.<sup>133</sup> After epidural injection, morphine reaches its peak concentration in plasma within 10 minutes, but this concentration is deficient and unable to provide clinically relevant analgesia.<sup>132,134</sup> Elimination half- life from cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) is comparable to that from plasma, but CSF concentrations are quite high following epidural injection; consequently, it takes 12 to 24 hours for them to drop to minimal effective concentrations (about 10 ng/mL).

In Table 74.5, the typical doses of neuraxial narcotics are listed. It is possible to utilize short- acting lipid- soluble opioids (fentanyl, sufentanil). As with adults, several injections or a continuous infusion are required to prolong postoperative pain treatment significantly. Their analgesic effect is primarily systemic, and the patient may experience acute respiratory depression (sudden apnea); this condition is very different from the progressive and delayed respiratory depression, preceded by pruritus, sedation, and bradypnea, that has been reported after excessive doses of epidural and intrathecal morphine.

Epinephrine 5 mg/L or 1/200,000 concentration is frequently co- administered with local anesthetics to decrease plasma peak concentration<sup>113</sup> and prolong the duration of the blockade, especially in children younger than four years

<!-- PDF page 14 -->

**Table 74.5** Pediatric Regional Anesthesia Additives and Recommended Doses

|**Additive**|**Recommended**<br>**Doses**|**Maximum**<br>**Doses**|
|---|---|---|
|Morphine<br>intrathecal<br>epidural|10 mcg/kg<br>30 mcg/kg|20 mcg/kg<br>50 mcg/kg|
|Fentanyl (epidural)|1–1.5 mcg/kg|2.5 mcg/kg|
|Sufentanil (epidural)|0.25–0.5 mcg/kg|0.75 mcg/kg|
|Clonidine (epidural or<br>peripheral nerve)|1–1.5 mcg/kg|2 mcg/kg|
|Ketamine*(epidural or<br>peripheral nerve)|0.5 mg/kg|1 mg/kg|
|Dexmedetomidine (peripheral<br>nerve)|1–1.5 mcg/kg|2 mcg/kg|


*Preservative- free ketamine (preferably preservative- free _S_ - ketamine). Most additives and adjuvants are used off- label.

of age.<sup>135,136</sup> Another benefit expected from epinephrine is early detection of accidental intravenous injection (test dose) because young children are susceptible to the arrhythmogenic properties of epinephrine. Neuraxial epinephrine has long been suspected of eliciting spinal ischemia potentially: even though this fear proved to be unfounded, many anesthesiologists recommend using lower concentrations of epinephrine (2.5 mg/L or 1/400,000) in local anesthetic solutions administered to neonates and infants; at such concentrations, the absorption rate of caudal bupivacaine is decreased by 25%.<sup>136</sup>

Clonidine, like epinephrine, is an α2- adrenergic agonist that offers several benefits in children when added to local anesthetics either neuraxially<sup>137–139</sup> or peripherally (see Table 74.5)<sup>140</sup> ; it increases (by a factor of ∼2) the duration of nerve blockade without eliciting hemodynamic disorders, decreases plasma peak concentration of the local anesthetics, and produces slight sedation for 1 to 3 hours postoperatively (which does not preclude hospital discharge). The addition of clonidine often eliminates the need for the placement of a catheter to prolong postoperative pain relief, thus reducing morbidity and costs. However, its clearance in neonates is approximately one- third of that in adults owing to immature elimination pathways<sup>141</sup> and several instances of respiratory depression in neonates and small infants have been reported<sup>142,143</sup> ; this additive should be avoided during the first six months of life.<sup>144</sup>

Dexmedetomidine, another α2- adrenoceptor agonist, has been used as an adjunct to prolong peripheral nerve blocks.<sup>145</sup> In children, a 0.5- to 1- g/kg dosage provides analgesia for up to 8 hours after surgery.<sup>146</sup> Considering the lack of data on the toxicity of these drugs in children, it is essential that they be given at the safest possible dose. The risks of bradycardia and sedation must be weighed against the benefits for children with compromised health.

Ketamine, especially _S-_ ketamine, is an exciting adjuvant because of its blocking effects on _N-_ methyl- d- aspartate receptors and interaction with sodium channels in a local anesthetic–like fashion (it shares a binding site with local anesthetics). Co- administered at a dose of 0.25 to 0.5 mg/kg,

ketamine prolongs the duration of analgesia for many hours<sup>138,147</sup> with no significant adverse effects. However, this is not approved for use in the United States for this indication.

Many other agents have been occasionally used as adjuvants to local anesthetics.<sup>148</sup> Although some of them proved to have analgesic properties (corticosteroids, buprenorphine, neostigmine, tramadol, and midazolam), they all produce significant adverse effects that preclude their use in most patients. Furthermore, their administration to pediatric patients raises ethical questions, and they are not approved for pediatric use in the United States.

### CLINICAL APPLICATION AND SELECTION OF LOCAL ANESTHETIC

In addition to considering the pharmacologic implications of a local anesthetic and its adjuvants, additional factors must be considered when selecting a local anesthetic for a child, as regional techniques are typically used for analgesia rather than anesthetic purposes. Consideration must be given to (1) the site and severity of surgery, (2) the anticipated duration of intense postoperative pain, and (3) hospitalization versus early discharge. Tables 74.6 and 74.7 list common dosages and their volume.

For outpatient surgery, lidocaine, chloroprocaine, and mepivacaine are preferred anesthetics. Ropivacaine, levobupivacaine, and bupivacaine are frequently used for inpatient surgery. l- Enantiomers are well- known for their lower cardiac toxicity compared to bupivacaine and are now recommended for use, particularly in continuous infusions.<sup>149</sup> In contrast to bupivacaine, the pharmacologic properties of ropivacaine permit differential nerve blockage<sup>150</sup> and limit myotoxicity.<sup>151</sup> The plasma concentration of ropivacaine reaches its maximum later than bupivacaine, sometimes up to 2 hours after injection.<sup>124</sup> Several pediatric studies have shown that delaying the peak plasma concentration of ropivacaine reduces the maximum plasma concentration and systemic toxicity risk.<sup>124,152</sup> Even though plasma concentrations of free and total ropivacaine are higher in younger children, plasma concentrations of ropivacaine and its principal metabolite (2,6- pipecoloxylidide) are unaffected by the duration of local anesthetic infusion. However, ropivacaine epidural infusion should not be maintained for longer than 36 hours in infants younger than three months.<sup>153</sup> The clearance of ropivacaine increases with age but remains constant throughout the infusion in all age groups. Ropivacaine appears to be more predictable and safer during the continuous infusion for 48 to 72 hours than bupivacaine; the plasma concentration of bupivacaine increases, and clearance decreases proportionally to the duration of the infusion.<sup>106</sup> Rarely have pediatric studies examined the pharmacokinetics of local anesthetics continuously administered perineurally. The safety of continuous regional anesthesia techniques in children depends on using low- concentration solutions accompanied by low plasma concentrations of local anesthetics to reduce the risk of local anesthetic systemic toxicity. Adjuvants like clonidine or ketamine enhance the quality and duration of the blockade without preventing an early hospital discharge. In many instances, these medications render

unnecessary the insertion of a catheter and establishment of a continuous infusion for postoperative pain relief.

Continuous epidural anesthesia was the only option for treating persistent pain for a long time. Recently, peripheral nerve catheter techniques have shown efficacy<sup>13</sup> with less morbidity and limitations than continuous epidural blockade, allowing for hospital discharge<sup>29</sup> and management at home in selected pediatric patients. Low- concentration (0.1%–0.2%) levobupivacaine or ropivacaine (continuous infusion 2–5 mL/h or on- demand injections 2–5 mL) is the best and safest option for these procedures.

Patient- controlled continuous infusion may reduce local anesthetic doses in children while maintaining analgesia.<sup>154,155</sup> Duflo and associates compared standard continuous administration (0.1 mL/kg/h) and patient- controlled regional anesthesia regimen (infusion of 0.02 mL/kg/h and a bolus of 0.1 mL/kg every 30 minutes) of 0.2% ropivacaine in patients undergoing fascia iliaca compartment or sciatic catheters. Patient- controlled administration had lower hourly consumption. This group had lower ropivacaine plasma levels than standard administration (0.31 and 0.86 mg/mL at 24 hours and 0.31 and 0.52 at 48 hours). A recent study compared plasma ropivacaine levels with continuous epidural and psoas compartment blocks in children.<sup>156</sup> The continuous psoas compartment block used 0.2 mg/kg/h ropivacaine infusion. This patient group

had ropivacaine plasma concentrations below 0.59 mcg/ mL, lower than the epidural catheter group. According to recent studies, home bupivacaine infusions in adolescents with indwelling catheters were safe.<sup>157</sup>

Regional techniques involving ultrasound- guided interfascial plane blocks are gaining popularity (see Truncal Blocks, below). In contrast to other peripheral nerve blocks, most experts believe that maximum local anesthetic volume is crucial for interfascial plane block analgesia. However, in patients with underlying cardiac conduction defects or those at risk for postoperative conduction anomalies, local anesthetic toxicity from large volumes of local anesthetic is a significant concern. Providing adequate volumes of local anesthetic through bilateral catheters while avoiding the risk of the local anesthetic’s systemic toxicity can be particularly difficult in neonates, underweight, or organ- dysfunctional patients (LAST). An **a** lternating- **s** ide **p** rogrammed **i** ntermittent **re** peated (ASPIRe) bolus regimen was described to address this issue.<sup>158</sup>

ASPIRe bolus regimen (see Table 74.7) is a simple but practical dosing approach that permits clinicians to use larger volumes of a local anesthetic to achieve analgesic efficacy while minimizing LAST risk in patients with bilateral interfascial plane catheters. Instead of bolusing every hour, a desired bolus volume of local anesthetic delivers every 120 minutes but staggered by 60 minutes into each
