**<mark>Fig. 74.65 Ultrasound longitudinal images of conus medullaris.</mark>**

<!-- PDF page 53 -->

placement. It is critical to use saline for the LOR technique to facilitate ultrasound imaging.

When the needle tip penetrates the epidural space, the LOR technique syringe is disconnected, and no reflux of biologic fluid (blood or CSF) should appear at the hub. The next step involves injecting the local anesthetic slowly through the epidural needle or a catheter. Progressive displacement of the dura mater can be visualized by placing an ultrasound probe in line with the spinous process line during injection in infants younger than two years of age.<sup>185</sup> Using ultrasound, the anatomy of the spinal canal, the position of the spinal cord, visualization of the ligamentum flavum, and the space caudal spinous processes can be seen (see Fig. 74.65).<sup>362</sup> When a catheter is inserted, it should not be introduced more than 3 cm to avoid buckling, knotting, and lateralization of blockade or erratic migration. Tunneling the catheter reduces the incidence of accidental removal and bacterial contamination.<sup>363</sup> Catheters inserted over a long distance must be controlled like caudal catheters (Fig. 74.66).

The volume of the anesthetic solution depends on the upper level of analgesia required for the completion of the surgery; approximately 0.1 mL/year of age is necessary to


![](assets/chapter-74/74._Regional_Anesthesia_in_Children.pdf-0053-04.png)


Cephalad Caudad<br>Epidural Space<br>Posterior dura mater<br>Spinal cord<br>Anterior dura mater<br>Epidural catheter<br>

**Fig. 74.66 Ultrasound longitudinal images of catheter in epidural space.**

block one neuromere.<sup>364</sup> Usual volumes of injectate range from 0.5 to 1 mL/kg (up to 20 mL), and the upper limit of sensory blockade ranges between T9 and T6 in more than 80% of patients.

Single- shot epidural blocks are appropriate for many pediatric surgeries, especially when adjuvants such as clonidine 1 to 2 mcg/kg and, in appropriate indications, morphine 30 mcg/kg or 10 mcg /kg of hydromorphone are co- administered. In addition, major operations resulting in long- lasting postoperative pain require the placement of an epidural catheter and postoperative infusion of local anesthetics (Table 74.11).

In mature children who understand the concept of patient- controlled analgesia and are willing to use it, patient- controlled epidural analgesia (PCEA) can be an exciting option. A prospective study involving 128 children older than five reported a 90.1% success rate; PCEA was stopped in 6.1% of children because of adverse effects and only 3.8% because of inadequate analgesia.<sup>365</sup> The local anesthetic was either 0.0625% or 0.125% bupivacaine with fentanyl 2 to 10 mcg/mL; the background infusion rate was 0.2 mL/kg/h or less, and 1- to 3- mL bolus doses were permitted every 15 to 30 minutes with a maximum amount of bupivacaine of 0.4 mg/kg/h.

Another prospective study involving 58 children (age range, 7–12 years) undergoing lower extremity orthopedic surgery compared continuous epidural infusion of 0.2% ropivacaine 0.2 mL/kg/h with PCEA, with background infusion of 1.6 mL/h and 2- mL bolus doses (lockout interval, 10 minutes) of the same solution. Pain scores were excellent and identical in both groups. Still, children from the PCEA group required half the doses of ropivacaine on an hourly basis compared to the continuous infusion group.<sup>154</sup>

**Thoracic Epidural Anesthesia.** Thoracic epidural blocks are indicated for major operations requiring long- lasting pain relief, thus requiring placement of an epidural catheter to allow repeat injections or continuous infusion of local anesthetic. These are not commonly used techniques in children because indications are limited to thoracic and upper abdominal surgery, and spinal cord damage is a risk. In children younger than one year, the procedure is similar to that for a lumbar approach, with needle insertion perpendicular

<!-- PDF page 54 -->

to the spinous process line because the spine displays a single flexure, especially when bent. As the patient grows and the flexure develops, the technique becomes progressively similar to thoracic approaches in adults, requiring cephalic orientation of the Tuohy needle up to a 45- degree angle to the skin. A paramedian approach can be used instead, but it is rarely required in children. In infants, ultrasonography makes visible the dura mater, the progression of the Tuohy needle, and, in many cases, the progression and final position of the tip of the epidural catheter.<sup>366</sup>

**Cervical Epidural Anesthesia.** No surgical indications exist for cervical epidural blockade in children. Very rarely, the technique can be contemplated for patients with chronic pain or to prevent phantom limb pain before an amputation of the upper arm at the scapular level (osteosarcoma of the humerus), which is done almost exclusively in adolescents. The block procedure is the same as in adults.

### SPINAL ANESTHESIA

### Anatomy and Physiology

Infants with a gestational age of less than one year have a lower spinal cord and dural sac terminal level than adults (see earlier section on Caudal Anesthesia). In addition, the volume of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) varies significantly from patient to patient based on their age, ranging from more than 10 mL/kg in neonates to 4 mL/kg in infants weighing less than 15 kg, to 3 mL/kg in children, to 1.5 to 2.0 mL/kg in adolescents and adults. In infants, for example, 50% of the CSF volume is found in the spinal subarachnoid space, whereas in adults, this percentage drops to 25%. In terms of pharmacokinetics, this has significant implications and helps to explain why higher doses of local anesthetics are needed for spinal anesthesia in infants and young children.

Infants’ CSF hydrostatic pressure is lower in the dorsal recumbent position<sup>367</sup> and further reduced under general anesthesia. Therefore, the needle is moved slowly during a spinal block to detect any CSF reflux before proceeding.

The effects of spinal anesthesia on children older than five are similar to those seen in adults. In contrast, even in the presence of cardiac malformations,<sup>368</sup> younger patients maintain hemodynamic stability with no evidence of profound hypotension or bradycardia.<sup>369</sup> However, in infants aged 1.5 to 5 months,<sup>370</sup> a drop in mean arterial pressure was reported during the first 10 minutes after injection of 0.8 mg/kg of 0.5% bupivacaine. Intravenous fluids quickly reversed this temporary drop in pressure with no adverse effects. Former preterm infants born at a postconceptional age of 41 weeks showed similar results, along with a concurrent decrease in cerebral blood flow.<sup>371</sup>

### Indications and Contraindications

Spinal anesthesia has limited indications in pediatric patients. One of these is inguinal hernia repair in former preterm infants younger than 60 weeks of postconceptual age,<sup>372,373</sup> who are prone to develop postoperative apnea after general anesthesia or even light sedation.<sup>374</sup> However, even after pure spinal anesthesia, apnea may occur postoperatively (as they may preoperatively), and it is prudent to keep at- risk infants hospitalized. Other indications

are scarce, mainly for elective lower abdominal or lower extremity surgery<sup>375–377</sup> and, occasionally, for cardiac surgery or cardiac catheterization,<sup>378,379</sup> but these indications are controversial. There has been a more significant interest in spinal anesthetics for infants lately due to the potential for neurocognitive changes associated with general anesthesia.<sup>380–382</sup>

### Technique

The spinal anesthesia technique is similar to lumbar puncture. It can be performed with the patient in the sitting (see Fig 74.1) or lateral decubitus position (see Fig. 74.60). Currently, hyperbaric tetracaine and bupivacaine are the most commonly used local anesthetics. Isobaric bupivacaine can be used instead.<sup>375</sup> Ropivacaine<sup>377</sup> and levobupivacaine<sup>383</sup> may become the future standard but are not currently approved for spinal administration in pediatric patients.

Ultrasound can image the infant’s spine well and makes ultrasound- guided spinal anesthesia possible.<sup>384</sup> Clinically, ultrasound for infant spinal anesthesia has limited but promising research. The effectiveness of the ultrasound on the success rate of infant spinal anesthesia is highly dependent on the sonographer’s experience level. Continuous ultrasound guidance instead of preprocedural ultrasound allows needle redirection toward a visible target, limited needle advancement into undesirable structures, and visualization of the injectate at the desired intrathecal space target.<sup>384</sup> However, ultrasound and landmark- based spinal anesthesia in infants have not been compared. Thus, further studies are needed to determine if ultrasound can improve infant spinal anesthesia success.

### Drugs and Doses

The usual doses are 0.5 to 0.8 mg/kg to achieve low to medium levels and 1 mg/kg to achieve high levels (T2–T4). The drugs most frequently used are tetracaine 0.5% and bupivacaine 0.5%. The block duration is approximately 60 to 75 minutes for both drugs. Newborns and infants are at an increased risk for toxicity after administering amide local anesthetics; the trouble is even more significant in the case of jaundice.<sup>385</sup> Usual spinal dosages for neonates to adolescents are given in Table 74.12.

The older the child, the lower the dose required; in children between 6 months and 14 years, 0.5% hyperbaric bupivacaine at a dose of 0.2 mg/kg has been used with a success rate of 98%. More recently, the successful use of 0.5% ropivacaine in children between 1 and 17 years at 0.5 mg/kg and 0.5% levobupivacaine in children between 1 and 14 years at 0.3 mg/kg has been reported. Clonidine 1 mcg/kg, fentanyl 1 mcg/kg, and morphine 4 to 5 mcg/ kg are reported in the pediatric literature as adjuvants to increase the spinal block duration (see Table 74.12). In cardiac patients, a higher dose of morphine has been used for spinal anesthesia to ensure adequate postoperative analgesia.

### Adverse Effects and Complications

Spinal anesthesia is technically tricky in neonates and infants; the failure rate ranges from 10% to 25%.<sup>374,386</sup> The blockade’s short duration and lack of residual analgesia are significant limitations, prompting frequent consideration of alternatives (awake caudal anesthesia) or additional
